# 32. Building an Elite Deadlift: Hinge Selection, Volume, Intensity, and Technical Constraints

## What "elite" means in this article

Elite, in this context, is not a number on the bar. It is the ability to express a near-maximal hinge under fatigue, with a repeatable bracing pattern, a stable bar path, and a predictable recovery curve from session to session. The athlete who hits a personal best once on a good day is not elite. The athlete who can warm into a heavy single inside thirty minutes, on a planned day, with a known RPE, is.

That framing dictates every choice that follows.

## Hinge selection: choosing the primary pattern

Most advanced lifters carry one true competition pull and one or two training hinges that load similar tissues without copying the main lift. The pattern hierarchy below is a routing tool, not a ranking.

### Conventional pull

Best primary when the lifter has long arms relative to torso, tolerates lumbar load well, and shows symmetric hip extension. Conventional rewards strong mid-back and lat tension at the floor.

### Sumo pull

Best primary when the lifter has strong hip abduction and external rotation, a shorter torso, and tolerates wide stance squatting. Sumo rewards strong adductors, glutes, and a vertical torso under heavy load.

### Trap bar pull

A primary option for hybrid athletes who do not compete in powerlifting, including grapplers who want a vertically loaded hinge with less low-back shear. Also a useful secondary for competition pullers who need to keep hinge volume up while reducing technical and lumbar tax.

### Block and deficit pulls

Block pulls reduce the demand at the floor and bias mid-range and lockout. Deficit pulls increase the demand at the floor and bias starting strength and patience off the ground. Choose one based on where the bar actually slows down, not where it feels heavy.

### Routing rule

If the bar consistently slows in the first six inches and the back rounds early, prioritize deficit work and lat-driven start cues. If the bar clears the knees and then stalls, prioritize block work, glute lockouts, and tempo pulls to the knee.

## Volume thinking

Deadlift volume behaves differently from squat or bench volume. The neural and connective tax per hard set is high, and the meaningful unit is not total reps but hard top sets across the week.

A practical framework:

- Hard top sets per week, defined as work above roughly 80 percent or RPE 8 and above, usually live in a range of two to six for an advanced lifter, depending on phase, lift, and recovery.
- Supplemental hinge volume sits below RPE 8, often in the 65 to 80 percent zone, and serves position, bar path, and bracing.
- Accessory hinge work is not deadlift volume. Romanian deadlifts, good mornings, and back extensions support the pull but should be tracked separately so the primary lift is not double-counted.

Junk volume on the deadlift shows up as repeated sets in the 70 to 80 percent zone with no clear position, tempo, or pause intent. Either add a constraint or drop the set.

## Intensity thinking

Advanced lifters generally need real exposure to loads above 85 percent of one rep max to maintain competition readiness, but not every week, and not at the same RPE.

A useful pattern is to rotate three intensity types across a meso:

- Heavy exposure: work at 87 to 95 percent for low reps, low to moderate frequency, used to test position and confidence.
- Speed and position: work at 65 to 80 percent with strict bar speed, tempo on the eccentric, or pauses just below the knee.
- Capacity: work at 70 to 82 percent for repeats, with longer rest, used to build tendon and connective tolerance.

Most weeks, only one of these should be the priority on the main pull. Stacking two priorities in one session is how advanced lifters quietly accumulate fatigue that does not show up until week three.

## Technical constraints

Technical constraints are not cues. They are conditions that must hold for the rep to count as a quality rep. Coaches enforce them. Athletes self-check them on video.

Examples by lift pattern:

- Conventional: bar stays in contact with the legs from the floor to lockout, hips and shoulders rise together, no early hip shoot.
- Sumo: knees track over the middle of the foot, hips do not rise faster than the bar, lockout is glute driven rather than lumbar driven.
- Trap bar: torso angle preserved through mid-range, no early shrug to start the pull.

If three reps in a set violate a technical constraint, the set is over. This is more useful than chasing the prescribed rep count.

## Current-to-goal gap thinking

Before choosing variations, the coach lays out the gap:

- Current best, current training max, and typical session top.
- Goal, expressed as a contest total or a tested single, with a date.
- Time available in training weeks, not calendar weeks.
- Recovery constraints: sleep floor, total weekly hard sessions, life stress.
- Pain constraints: any region where pain or restriction is currently limiting position or load tolerance.
- Technical limiter: where the bar actually slows, not the cue the athlete likes.

Only after that gap is mapped does the coach choose the primary hinge, the supplemental hinge, and the intensity mix.

## BJJ and hybrid athlete application

For a grappler, the deadlift is a posterior chain investment, not a sport skill. The goals shift:

- Maintain or improve a strong hinge without crashing grip and posterior chain recovery before hard mat days.
- Prefer trap bar or sumo for many grapplers who already accumulate lumbar load from defensive postures and stand-up grip fighting.
- Cap heavy exposures to one true heavy session per week in hard training blocks, and bias supplemental hinge work to Romanian deadlifts and back extensions.
- Schedule heavy hinge work away from hard rolling days when possible, ideally 24 to 48 hours of separation, recognizing this is a routing preference, not a guarantee of recovery.

## Substitutions

- If the lifter cannot tolerate the competition pull this week, regress to the closest pattern that maintains hip extension load: sumo to trap bar, conventional to block pulls from just below the knee.
- If grip is the limiter, hook grip practice on lighter sets, straps on supplemental work above a clear threshold (commonly the top supplemental sets), and dedicated grip work outside the session.
- If the lower back is the limiter, hinge volume shifts toward Romanian deadlifts, hip thrusts, and trap bar work for a defined block, and the coach reviews position before reloading the competition pull.

## Coach and clinician review triggers

Pause and review when any of the following appear:

- A new sharp or referred symptom during or after pulling, especially down a limb.
- A persistent loss of position that does not respond to standard cueing across two sessions.
- A drop in top-set performance of more than roughly 10 percent without a known stressor.
- Increasing morning stiffness lasting more than 30 to 60 minutes after pulling days.

Conservative routing: pause heavy hinge work, run a position-and-tempo week, and route to a qualified clinician if symptoms persist.

## Source-domain notes

Strength science, powerlifting practice, biomechanics of the hip and spine, autoregulation, and combat-sport interference management.

---

# 33. Deadlift Specialization Blocks: When to Pull Heavy and When to Build Around the Pull

## The specialization question

A deadlift specialization block is a defined period, usually four to eight weeks, where the deadlift gets first priority in fatigue allocation, exercise selection, and recovery planning. Everything else in the program is built to support it, or trimmed so it does not interfere.

Specialization is not a default mode. It is a choice with a cost. The cost is squat and bench progress slow or maintain, and total training stress on the posterior chain is elevated. The benefit is real technical and strength improvement on the pull.

## When to specialize on the deadlift

Specialize when several of the following are true:

- The deadlift is the lift with the largest gap between current and goal.
- The deadlift has stalled while squat and bench have continued to progress.
- A meet, test, or assessment is far enough out to absorb a focused block plus a normal peak.
- The athlete has the recovery bandwidth, sleep, and life stress profile to absorb heavier weekly posterior chain load.
- Technical issues at heavy loads are stable enough that more heavy exposure will help, rather than reinforcing a broken pattern.

If technique breaks down above 85 percent in a repeatable way, a specialization block built around heavy pulling will likely entrench the problem. Build around the pull first.

## When to build around the pull

Build around the pull, meaning keep deadlift volume modest and improve the surrounding system, when:

- The technical limiter is unresolved at heavy loads.
- The lower back, hips, or grip cannot currently tolerate higher hinge volume.
- Squat is also a priority lift and is progressing.
- The meet or test window is short enough that a true specialization block cannot fit before peaking.
- Recovery is compromised by life stress, sleep loss, or competing sport demands.

Building around the pull usually means one moderate deadlift session per week, two to three days of focused posterior chain accessory work, and aggressive attention to brace, lat tension, and hip position on supplemental movements.

## Block architecture for a deadlift specialization

A practical four to six week structure:

### Week 1 and 2: re-entry and position

- One heavier pull day, focused on technique under load, top sets at RPE 7 to 8.
- One supplemental hinge day, usually a paused or tempo variation at moderate load.
- Posterior chain accessories twice per week.

### Week 3 and 4: load

- Heavier pull day, top sets at RPE 8 to 9.
- Supplemental hinge day, slightly heavier than weeks 1 and 2 but still position-priority.
- Squat held at maintenance, usually one squat day with lower volume than baseline.

### Week 5: load test or overreach

- One heavy single at RPE 9, used as a checkpoint.
- Lower supplemental hinge volume that week.
- Optional second hinge exposure at moderate load if recovery allows.

### Week 6: deload or transition

- Reduce hinge volume by roughly half, keep one moderate exposure, and reassess.

This is a template, not a prescription. The coach adjusts based on the readiness signals discussed below.

## Fatigue triggers inside a block

Pull the brakes when:

- Bar speed on planned top sets drops two RPE points week over week at the same load.
- Sleep drops below the athlete's usual floor for more than two nights in the same week.
- Grip endurance collapses on supplemental work that was previously easy.
- Morning posterior chain stiffness persists past warm-up two sessions in a row.

Pulling the brakes usually means cutting one supplemental hinge exposure, not the main heavy session, unless the main heavy session is the source.

## Current-to-goal gap thinking

Before choosing a specialization block, the coach maps:

- Current numbers and recent training maxes on all three lifts.
- The goal pull, the time window, and the planned peak.
- Available recovery bandwidth, including any sport demands.
- Pain constraints that limit hinge tolerance.
- Technical limiter and whether more heavy exposure will help or hurt that limiter.

Specialization is justified when the gap is large, the timeline is long enough, recovery is available, pain is not actively limiting, and the technical limiter is stable.

## Application to BJJ and hybrid athletes

Most grapplers should rarely run a full deadlift specialization block during a competitive training cycle. The fatigue cost competes directly with mat performance.

Better routing:

- Off-season or post-competition windows are the place for a true specialization block.
- In-season, the focus is maintenance: one moderate pull day, posterior chain accessories, and grip work that does not double-tax the forearms.
- If a grappler insists on improving the pull during in-season training, accept that mat intensity must drop during the block.

## Substitutions inside a specialization block

- If conventional starts to break down, route the main session to trap bar or block pulls for two weeks while keeping supplemental volume on a competition-style hinge at lighter loads.
- If grip is the limiter, use straps on supplemental work and protect hook grip exposure for top sets.
- If lumbar tolerance drops mid-block, reduce range with elevated pulls for a week, hold position-focused supplemental work, and review before reloading.

## Coach and clinician review triggers

Pause and review when:

- A new sharp symptom appears mid-block.
- Top-set performance regresses two weeks running with no clear stressor.
- Pain at end of range becomes pain inside working range.

Conservative routing: pause heavy hinge work, hold supplemental at low intensity, and route to a clinician if symptoms persist.

## Source-domain notes

Strength science, powerlifting practice, periodization theory, autoregulation, and combat-sport interference management.

---

# 34. Squat Specialization for Advanced Lifters: Frequency, Variation, and Fatigue Control

## What squat specialization is

A squat specialization block raises squat frequency and quality, holds deadlift work at maintenance, and keeps bench progressing without competing for squat-day recovery. For most advanced lifters, this means squatting twice to four times per week with a clear hierarchy across sessions.

The block exists because, at advanced levels, the squat usually responds to frequency before it responds to more total weekly volume. More exposure to the pattern, at varied intensities, with intent on each rep, produces more technical and strength improvement than the same total volume packed into one or two sessions.

## When to specialize on the squat

Specialize when:

- The squat is the limiter for total or for sport-specific strength.
- Technique under load is reasonably stable at top sets, so more frequency does not entrench errors.
- The athlete can absorb more knee and hip load without flaring tendons or low-back tolerance.
- A meaningful timeline exists, usually six to ten weeks before a test, plus a peak.

## When not to specialize

Hold off if:

- Knee, hip, or low-back tolerance is currently a limiter at moderate loads.
- The technical pattern collapses at top sets in repeatable ways.
- Recovery is compromised by life stress or hard sport training.
- The deadlift is also stalled and needs first priority.

## Frequency design

A typical advanced squat specialization runs three squat days per week, structured by priority:

### Day A, primary

- One main squat variation at heavier loads, top sets at RPE 8 to 9.
- One or two back-off sets at reduced load with strict tempo or pause.

### Day B, secondary

- A squat variation that biases a weak point, often pause squat, tempo squat, or a different bar position.
- Top sets at RPE 7 to 8, with more total reps than Day A.

### Day C, technique and capacity

- Lighter squat variation at RPE 6 to 7, focused on position, brace, and bar speed.
- Higher set count, lower per-set intensity.

For some lifters, a fourth session is added at very low intensity for pattern work only, usually under 65 percent for a few sets of three to five.

## Variation choice

Variation is a tool, not a menu. Each variation must answer the question: what is this fixing?

- Pause squat: brace and control out of the hole, position under tension.
- Tempo squat: eccentric control, knee and hip coordination, hypertrophy of the working tissue.
- High bar squat: more knee and quad load, used when quads are the limiter.
- Low bar squat: more hip and posterior chain load, used when the back angle is the limiter.
- Front squat: upper back, brace, and quad position.
- Safety bar squat: upper back load reduced, used when shoulder or elbow tolerance is a constraint.

Choose two to three variations per block and hold them. Rotating variations every week prevents both adaptation and useful feedback.

## Volume thinking

Across the week, hard squat sets above roughly 80 percent or RPE 8 usually sit in a range of four to ten for an advanced lifter, with the rest of the volume distributed between supplemental and capacity work. The main and secondary days carry most of the hard work.

Junk volume on the squat is high-rep, mid-percentage sets with no tempo, pause, or position constraint. Either constrain the set or drop it.

## Fatigue control

Squat fatigue accumulates quietly. The signs:

- Warm-ups feel heavier than usual at the same weight.
- Knee or hip stiffness lasts longer into the session.
- Bar speed at the top set drops without the load changing.
- Sleep quality starts to slip after the main squat day.

When two or more show up in the same week, cut one supplemental squat exposure or reduce intensity on the secondary day for a week. Do not cut the primary heavy session first, unless it is the source.

## Current-to-goal gap thinking

Before choosing the block, map:

- Current squat numbers across variations, not just one rep max.
- Goal squat, expressed as a tested single or a contest expectation, with a date.
- Recovery bandwidth, including sleep, life stress, and any sport load.
- Pain constraints: any joint or region currently limiting squat tolerance.
- Technical limiter: where the bar slows, where position breaks, and whether that limiter is more about strength or about pattern.

The variations chosen, and the frequency, follow that map.

## Application to BJJ and hybrid athletes

Grapplers can use a modified squat emphasis in off-season windows. In-season:

- One main squat day per week, with a position-priority second exposure at lower intensity.
- Bias variations that reduce spinal load when grip and posterior chain fatigue is already high, such as safety bar or front squat.
- Avoid stacking heavy squat days against the hardest rolling sessions when possible.

## Substitutions

- If knee tolerance drops, reduce range with box squats or tempo work to a higher box for one to two weeks, and review before reloading full-range work.
- If hip tolerance drops, narrow stance or rotate to front squat for a defined window.
- If upper back tolerance is the limiter, route to safety bar or front squat to keep frequency without reloading the bar across the upper back.

## Coach and clinician review triggers

Pause and review when:

- A new sharp pain appears in the knee, hip, or low back.
- Position collapses repeatedly at loads previously controlled.
- Stiffness no longer responds to warm-up.
- Sleep and mood markers stay depressed across multiple weeks.

Conservative routing: drop intensity, hold pattern work only, and route to a clinician if symptoms persist.

## Source-domain notes

Strength science, powerlifting practice, biomechanics of the knee and hip, autoregulation, and combat-sport interference management.

---

# 35. Bench Press Specialization: Pressing Volume, Shoulder Tolerance, and Lockout Strength

## What bench specialization is

A bench specialization block raises bench frequency, structures pressing volume by purpose, and protects the shoulder so the higher frequency does not become a tolerance problem. Squat and deadlift hold at maintenance. The block runs four to eight weeks for most advanced lifters.

Bench responds well to frequency, but only inside a shoulder tolerance ceiling that is highly individual. Pushing past that ceiling usually produces a stall and a flare, not strength.

## When to specialize on the bench

Specialize when:

- Bench is the limiter for total or for upper-body sport demand.
- Shoulder, elbow, and wrist tolerance is currently good at the lifter's normal volume.
- A test or meet is far enough out to absorb a focused block plus a peak.
- Pressing variations can be rotated without flaring any joint.

## When not to specialize

Hold off if:

- Shoulder tolerance is currently limiting volume even at maintenance.
- Elbow tendinopathy is active.
- Wrist or pec tolerance has flared inside the last training block.
- Life stress is high and sleep is short.

## Frequency design

A typical advanced bench specialization runs three to four pressing days per week:

### Day A, primary

- Competition-style bench, top sets at RPE 8 to 9.
- Back-off sets at reduced load with strict tempo or pause.

### Day B, secondary

- Variation that biases a weak point: close grip, paused, spoto, or a board press.
- Top sets at RPE 7 to 8 with more total reps.

### Day C, volume

- Lighter pressing at RPE 6 to 7, often with a slightly different grip or bar.
- Higher set count, focused on bar path and shoulder position.

### Day D, optional accessory press

- Dumbbell pressing or incline pressing at moderate load.
- Used when shoulder tolerance is high and total pressing capacity needs to come up.

## Variation choice

- Paused bench: bar control, tightness off the chest, leg drive timing.
- Spoto press: brief pause just above the chest, used to build position at the hardest point of the bar path.
- Close grip bench: triceps and lockout, also reduces shoulder load for some lifters.
- Board press: lockout and overload above the sticking point.
- Larsen press, feet up: removes leg drive, builds upper back stability and pec contribution.
- Dumbbell bench: shoulder-friendly volume, range of motion, and stabilizer work.

Pick two pressing variations besides the main lift, hold them, and let them tell you something.

## Pressing volume

Hard pressing sets per week, above roughly 80 percent or RPE 8 on the main lift, typically range from six to twelve in a bench block for an advanced lifter, distributed across the main day and secondary day. The volume day adds capacity but should not push the main and secondary days into fatigue debt.

## Shoulder tolerance

The single biggest risk in a bench block is treating shoulders like the bar and pec only. Shoulder tolerance is the gating factor. Practical levers:

- Upper back volume two to three times per week, especially horizontal pulling and external rotation work.
- Bar path checked on video periodically to confirm the bar is not drifting forward at the top.
- Grip width and elbow flare adjusted to keep the shoulder in a tolerable arc.
- Pressing variations rotated to avoid the same shoulder arc for the entire block.

If the shoulder talks during warm-ups, the warm-up is information. Either change the variation that day or shorten the session.

## Lockout strength

Lockout strength is rarely a true triceps weakness. More often it is:

- A bar that drifts forward at the top because the upper back gave up.
- A bar that drifts back because leg drive is timed late.
- A genuine triceps gap, fixable with close grip and board work.

The coach diagnoses by watching the bar path, not by adding triceps volume.

## Current-to-goal gap thinking

Map:

- Current bench numbers across the planned variations.
- Goal bench with a date.
- Shoulder tolerance history.
- Pressing volume the lifter can currently absorb without flares.
- Technical limiter, identified from bar path video on top sets.

The block design follows this map.

## Application to BJJ and hybrid athletes

For grapplers:

- Pressing is a secondary or tertiary priority during competitive training.
- Bias pressing variations that protect the shoulder under high grip and frame fatigue: close grip, dumbbell, and floor press for some athletes.
- Cap weekly hard pressing exposures to one to two and load the rest as moderate volume.
- Pulling volume usually rises with pressing volume to protect shoulder position.

## Substitutions

- If the shoulder flares, drop the main bench day to one moderate exposure and rotate to floor press or dumbbell pressing for one to two weeks before reloading.
- If wrists flare, narrow grip or rotate to a neutral grip variation.
- If elbows flare, reduce close grip work, check wrist position, and review before reloading triceps-biased work.

## Coach and clinician review triggers

Pause and review when:

- A new sharp shoulder or pec symptom appears during or after pressing.
- Bar path collapses repeatedly at loads previously controlled.
- Pain at end of range becomes pain inside working range.

Conservative routing: pause heavy pressing, hold position and capacity work only, and route to a clinician if symptoms persist.

## Source-domain notes

Strength science, powerlifting practice, shoulder biomechanics, autoregulation, and upper-body tendinopathy management.

---

# 36. Overhead Press Development: Strict Press, Push Press, and Shoulder Capacity

## Why overhead matters for advanced athletes

The overhead press is not a competition lift in powerlifting, but it remains a high-value lift for advanced lifters and hybrid athletes. It builds shoulder strength through a long arc, reinforces brace and stack under load, and supports lockout strength on the bench. For grapplers and other combat athletes, overhead capacity supports posture and frame integrity.

A serious overhead program treats the strict press, the push press, and shoulder capacity work as three different jobs.

## Strict press: the position lift

The strict press is the slowest, most honest version of overhead. It exposes brace, hip and torso stack, and shoulder mobility in a way no other press does.

Programming notes:

- Treat the strict press like a heavy compound. Top sets between three and six reps at RPE 7 to 9, two to three times per week if it is the priority.
- Pause briefly at the shoulder before each rep on a portion of sets, to remove any stretch-shortening contribution and load the working tissue.
- Bar path is vertical from rack to lockout, ribs stacked over pelvis, no leaning back beyond a small natural finish.

## Push press: the bridge lift

The push press uses leg drive to launch the bar past the sticking point. It is useful for two reasons: it allows heavier overhead loading than the strict press, and it trains rapid lower-body to upper-body force transfer.

Programming notes:

- Use push press when the goal is overhead strength under heavier load than the strict press can support.
- Top sets between two and five reps at RPE 7 to 9.
- The dip is shallow, vertical, and fast. A deep, slow dip is a front squat, not a push press.
- The lockout still has to be a real lockout. If the bar travels forward, the rep does not count.

## Shoulder capacity work

Shoulder capacity work is the volume that supports the main lifts. It is not optional, and it is not a warm-up.

Useful categories:

- Vertical pressing accessories: half-kneeling press, single-arm dumbbell press, Z press.
- Horizontal pulling: rows in volumes equal to or greater than pressing volume across the week.
- External rotation work: band, cable, or dumbbell, two to three exposures per week.
- Scapular work: serratus push-ups, prone Y and T raises, scapular pull-ups.

Capacity work is dosed by tolerance, not by template. If a particular accessory consistently triggers a flare, replace it. Do not push through.

## Volume thinking

Across the week, hard overhead sets above roughly 80 percent or RPE 8 typically sit between three and eight for an advanced lifter when overhead is a priority. Capacity work runs in higher rep ranges at lower percentages.

## Decision rules

- If the goal is competition powerlifting and the bench is the priority, overhead work is supplemental. One to two exposures per week, kept moderate.
- If the goal is hybrid performance, overhead work can carry equal weight to bench, with two to three exposures per week.
- If the shoulder is currently sensitive, overhead frequency drops first, before bench frequency.

## Current-to-goal gap thinking

Map:

- Current strict press and push press numbers.
- Goal, expressed as a strict press number, a push press number, or a sport demand.
- Shoulder tolerance history.
- Pressing and pulling volume the lifter currently absorbs.
- Technical limiter: brace, mobility, or pure strength.

## Application to BJJ and hybrid athletes

Overhead work is particularly useful for grapplers, because it directly supports posture and frame strength. Practical notes:

- Use the strict press as the priority overhead lift, two times per week at moderate to heavy intensity.
- Push press is optional and useful only if technique is clean, especially the dip.
- Z press and half-kneeling press are excellent for athletes whose ribs flare or who lose stack under load.
- Capacity work for the shoulder is essential at higher mat volumes.

## Substitutions

- If wrists are the limiter, rotate to neutral grip dumbbell pressing.
- If the standing position is the limiter, rotate to half-kneeling or seated pressing for a defined window.
- If the shoulder is mildly sensitive but workable, reduce range with landmine pressing for one to two weeks.

## Coach and clinician review triggers

Pause and review when:

- A new sharp shoulder or neck symptom appears during or after pressing.
- Range of motion at the shoulder drops noticeably session to session.
- Pain that previously appeared at end of range now appears mid-range.

Conservative routing: pause heavy overhead, hold capacity work only at tolerable load, and route to a clinician if symptoms persist.

## Source-domain notes

Strength science, shoulder biomechanics, overhead athlete practice, autoregulation, and combat-sport posture demands.

---

# 37. Heavy Singles in Training: When to Use Them Without Burning Out

## What a heavy single is for

A heavy single is a training tool with a specific job: confirming that a load is movable on a given day, at a given technical standard, with a known RPE. It is not a personal record attempt, and it is not the most efficient way to build strength.

Used correctly, heavy singles preserve neural readiness, give the coach and athlete a clean data point, and reduce the cost of grinding for fives or eights at near-maximal loads. Used poorly, they accumulate central nervous system fatigue with little corresponding adaptation.

## When to use heavy singles

Use heavy singles when:

- The athlete is advanced enough that bar speed and technical execution at near-max are stable.
- A measurable check on readiness is useful, such as before a test or meet.
- Volume work has built capacity and the question is whether that capacity has converted to peak strength.
- A specific weight needs to be rehearsed for confidence reasons.

Avoid heavy singles when:

- Technique under load is unstable or actively breaking down.
- Recovery is compromised by sleep, life stress, or hard sport training.
- The athlete is early in a block, where exposure to maximal loads will not yet produce a useful signal.
- The lifter cannot stop on RPE and will turn the single into a maximal attempt.

## Frequency and dosing

A practical framework:

- One heavy single per week on one lift, rotated across a block, is a sustainable load for most advanced lifters.
- Two heavy singles per week, on different lifts, is the upper limit for most athletes in a focused block, and only when sleep and life stress are good.
- Heavy singles on three lifts in the same week are reserved for peaking weeks and tested infrequently outside that context.

The single is not the only work that day. Supplemental volume follows the single, usually at reduced intensity, and serves recovery and pattern.

## RPE targets

Heavy singles in training typically live at RPE 8 to 9. RPE 10 singles are rare, and should be reserved for tested checkpoints or contest situations.

A useful rule: the single ends when bar speed slows below the threshold the coach and athlete have agreed in advance, not when the athlete decides on the platform whether to grind.

## Decision rules and routing

If the planned single misses on the first attempt, the coach has three routes:

- If technique was clean and bar speed was simply too slow, drop the load by five to ten percent and complete a working single. The block continues normally next week.
- If technique broke down, end the heavy work for the day and review the video. Do not retry at the same load.
- If something feels off in a way that does not match recent training, pause the heavy work, complete moderate supplemental work or end the session, and reassess later.

## Fatigue triggers

Pull heavy singles from the week when:

- Bar speed on warm-up sets is noticeably slower than baseline.
- Heart rate or perceived exertion at warm-up loads is higher than usual.
- Sleep dropped below the athlete's floor in the last two nights.
- Mood and focus are flat in a way the coach recognizes as a fatigue signal.

Replacing the single with a doubles or triples at the planned single's RPE minus one is a common substitution.

## Current-to-goal gap thinking

Map:

- Current top training single across the relevant lift.
- Goal single, expressed as a tested single or contest attempt, with a date.
- Frequency of heavy singles in the current block.
- Recovery and life-stress context.
- Technical stability at the planned load.

The frequency and load of heavy singles in the block follow this map.

## Application to BJJ and hybrid athletes

For grapplers, heavy singles are a low-frequency, high-value tool. Practical notes:

- Use one heavy single per week at most during in-season training, on the lift the athlete cares most about maintaining.
- Avoid heavy singles in the 48 hours before a hard rolling day or competition simulation.
- Bias trap bar or sumo singles for grapplers who tolerate them better than conventional.
- Heavy singles serve mostly as readiness checks rather than progression drivers during hard mat blocks.

## Substitutions

- Replace a planned heavy single with a triple at the planned weight minus a known percentage when bar speed is clearly down.
- Replace a planned heavy single with a paused or tempo version of the same lift at lower load when position is the question.
- Replace a planned heavy single with a constraint variation (block pull, paused squat, paused bench) when fatigue is high but training stimulus is still needed.

## Coach and clinician review triggers

Pause and review when:

- A new sharp symptom appears at heavy loads that did not appear at moderate loads.
- Technique fails in a new way that did not show up in volume work.
- Repeated heavy singles result in extended recovery times, more than two days of meaningful soreness or stiffness.

Conservative routing: pause heavy singles for one to two weeks, run volume and position work, and route to a clinician if symptoms persist.

## Source-domain notes

Strength science, powerlifting practice, autoregulation, RPE methodology, and combat-sport interference management.

---

# 38. Weak Point Training for Powerlifting: Identifying the Real Limiter

## What a weak point actually is

A weak point is the specific position, range, or pattern where the bar consistently slows or fails on the heaviest sets that still represent the lift in question. It is not the muscle group that feels sore the next day, the lift the athlete dislikes, or the position that looks ugly on video.

Coaching the wrong weak point is the most common cause of slow advanced lifters. The athlete adds triceps volume for a lockout problem that is actually a bar path problem, or adds glute work for a deadlift starting strength problem that is actually a lat tension problem.

## How to identify the real limiter

Identification requires three sources of evidence:

### Video on top sets

Watch the heaviest sets, not the working sets. The position that breaks at RPE 9 is the position that limits the lift. Working sets often look better than top sets, and looking only at working sets hides the limiter.

### Bar speed on top sets

A clean bar accelerates through mid-range and decelerates only as it nears lockout or the chest. A bar that decelerates early, or that stalls and then moves again, is telling you where the limiter sits. Track this visually or with a velocity tool when available.

### Failure patterns

Where do missed reps actually fail? In the bench, do they fail off the chest, at mid-range, or at lockout? In the squat, do they fail in the hole, mid-range, or at the top? In the deadlift, do they fail off the floor, at the knee, or at lockout?

Three sources, looking for agreement, identify the real limiter.

## Categories of limiter and standard responses

### Bench

- Off the chest: paused bench, spoto press, larger upper back and pec mass.
- Mid-range: paused bench at higher loads, close grip variations, upper back tightness work.
- Lockout: close grip bench, board press, triceps accessory work, and bar path review.

### Squat

- Out of the hole: pause squat, tempo squat, more total quad and hip mass.
- Mid-range: tempo squat, larger posterior chain, brace work under heavier loads.
- Lockout: less common as a true limiter; usually a back angle or hip extension problem better addressed by good mornings and hip thrust work.

### Deadlift

- Off the floor: deficit pulls, lat tension cues, slow eccentric Romanian deadlifts, glute and quad development for the start position.
- At the knee: tempo pulls to the knee, paused pulls below the knee, posterior chain hypertrophy.
- Lockout: block pulls, glute bridges, hip thrusts, upper back work, and lockout-focused accessories.

These are starting points, not formulas. The video and bar speed evidence dictates the choice.

## The hidden limiter: tolerance

Sometimes the real limiter is not strength or position. It is tolerance for higher loads in a region the athlete protects subconsciously. A lifter with a history of low-back sensitivity may pull back from a brace at high loads in a way that looks like a weak posterior chain.

When the obvious strength interventions are not changing the bar speed at top sets, consider tolerance as the limiter and route accordingly.

## Decision rules for programming the weak point

- Weak point work is added to the program, not substituted for the main lift. The main lift still drives strength.
- Weak point work usually carries two to four hard sets per week, often at moderate intensity with a constraint, such as pause, tempo, or partial range.
- Weak point work is held for at least four weeks before judging it. Tissue and pattern do not change in two weeks.
- Weak point work is removed when the bar speed or position evidence at top sets confirms the change.

## Current-to-goal gap thinking

Map:

- Current numbers and the failure pattern at the top of those numbers.
- Goal numbers, with the failure pattern that would be expected at those numbers based on the current pattern.
- Technical limiter evidence from video and bar speed.
- Tolerance constraints that may be hiding behind the strength picture.
- Time available to hold weak point work for a meaningful block.

## Application to BJJ and hybrid athletes

For grapplers, weak point work is still useful, but the cost-benefit shifts:

- Identify limiters that also matter for sport, such as posterior chain strength for the deadlift and posture-supporting press patterns.
- Avoid stacking high-volume weak point work on top of high mat volume.
- Use lower-intensity, position-focused variations more than heavy weak point work.

## Substitutions

- If the planned weak point variation flares a joint, rotate to a related variation at a lower demand. Spoto press becomes paused bench at moderate load, deficit pull becomes paused pull below the knee.
- If the athlete cannot tolerate the demand of the weak point work in the current block, hold the main lift quality and reschedule the weak point block for the next phase.

## Coach and clinician review triggers

Pause and review when:

- The weak point intervention triggers pain that was not present before.
- The intervention fails to change top-set evidence after a full block of work.
- The athlete loses position on the main lift during the weak point block.

Conservative routing: pause the weak point work, reassess the limiter, and route to a clinician if symptoms persist.

## Source-domain notes

Strength science, powerlifting practice, bar speed analysis, biomechanics, and autoregulation.

---

# 39. Powerlifting Peak Week: Tapering Volume Without Losing Readiness

## What peaking is and is not

Peak week is not the week the athlete gets stronger. It is the week the athlete recovers from the last block while maintaining the neural pattern needed to express maximum strength on meet day. Strength gain ended one to three weeks earlier. Peak week is about converting accumulated capacity into a single competition performance.

The two failure modes are familiar:

- Too much volume in the final week and the athlete steps on the platform tired.
- Too little exposure in the final week and the athlete steps on the platform out of pattern.

The job is to land between those two.

## The general shape of peak week

A practical structure for a competition on Saturday, with adjustments by lifter:

### Monday or Tuesday: openers or near openers

- Squat opener at RPE 7 to 8, or a single at roughly 90 to 93 percent of the planned opener.
- Bench opener at similar effort.
- Very limited supplemental volume after these singles.

### Wednesday: light technical work

- Light squat, bench, and pulling at submaximal loads, focused on bar speed and position.
- No top sets.

### Thursday: deadlift opener or near opener

- Deadlift opener at RPE 7 to 8, or a single at roughly 90 to 93 percent of the planned opener.
- Minimal supplemental work.

### Friday: rest or very light movement

- Walking, easy stretching, mobility work the athlete already responds well to.
- No new modalities.

### Saturday: meet

This is a template. Strong lifters with longer recovery curves benefit from earlier opener exposure and a longer rest tail. Lifters who lose pattern quickly benefit from later, lighter exposure.

## Volume tapering across the final two weeks

A practical pattern:

- Two weeks out: volume reduced by roughly 30 to 50 percent compared to the previous high week, intensity preserved on top sets.
- One week out: volume reduced again, often to half of the two-weeks-out volume, with top sets backed off in intensity.
- Peak week: as above, with the openers serving as the main intensity exposure.

The exact numbers depend on the lifter's history with peak weeks. Past peak weeks are the best data the coach has.

## Maintaining readiness without adding fatigue

Readiness has three dimensions to maintain in peak week:

- Neural readiness: short exposure to near-opener loads keeps the central nervous system primed.
- Technical readiness: light, clean reps at submaximal loads keep the pattern alive.
- Physical readiness: sleep, hydration, and travel logistics are handled so the athlete is not depleted on meet morning.

Anything that does not contribute to one of these three dimensions does not belong in peak week.

## Decision rules

- If the opener single in peak week feels heavier than expected, do not chase a second attempt. Trust the taper and let the recovery tail do its work.
- If the opener single feels fast and easy, do not be tempted to add a heavier single. The pattern is confirmed; protect the readiness.
- If a small flare appears in peak week (a tight low back, a sensitive shoulder), reduce or remove the supplemental work and protect the main openers.

## Fatigue triggers and routing

Cut volume further or remove a planned exposure when:

- Sleep drops below the athlete's floor for two consecutive nights.
- Resting heart rate or readiness markers are noticeably elevated.
- The athlete reports unusual stiffness or sensitivity on warm-up.

Routing in this situation is almost always toward less, not toward more.

## Current-to-goal gap thinking

Map:

- Recent top sets on each lift over the last four to six weeks.
- Planned openers, second attempts, and third attempts.
- Recovery curve from past peak weeks.
- Any current pain or tolerance issue.
- Travel, weight cut, and life stress in the peak week itself.

The peak week structure follows that map, biased toward the conservative side when the evidence is mixed.

## Application to BJJ and hybrid athletes

Most grapplers are not peaking for a powerlifting meet, but the same principles apply when peaking for a grappling competition with a strength component, or for a tested lift that supports sport.

- Apply the same shape: openers earlier in the week, light technical work mid-week, full rest before competition.
- Reduce mat volume in the same arc, with the final hard rolls happening earlier in the week and being replaced by lighter, technique-only work near competition.
- Avoid introducing new strength stimuli in peak week.

## Substitutions

- If a planned opener feels off, drop ten percent and complete the rep at the lower load. Do not chase.
- If a lift cannot be performed cleanly in peak week, route to a constrained version at lighter load, such as a paused squat at moderate load instead of a planned competition squat single, and protect meet day.

## Coach and clinician review triggers

Pause and review when:

- New sharp symptoms appear in peak week.
- A planned opener cannot be completed at an RPE that should have been comfortable.
- Recovery markers do not stabilize across the week.

Conservative routing: reduce peak week exposures, communicate openly with the athlete about attempt selection, and consider scratching if a clinician's input is needed and unavailable.

## Source-domain notes

Strength science, powerlifting practice, taper and peaking theory, autoregulation, and competition logistics.

---

# 40. Attempt Selection for Powerlifting: Choosing Openers, Seconds, and Thirds

## What attempt selection is for

Attempt selection has one job: maximize the athlete's competition total at the appropriate level of risk. A meet is not a training day. The right attempt is the heaviest weight the athlete will make under the technical standard of the federation, on that day, with the warm-ups already in their legs.

Coaches who choose attempts to feel brave usually total less than coaches who choose attempts to score points.

## The opener

The opener is the lift the athlete is certain to make on the worst day they could plausibly have.

Practical guidelines:

- The opener is typically the heaviest weight the athlete has hit cleanly for a double in training within the last four to six weeks, or roughly 90 to 93 percent of the planned third attempt, depending on the lift and the lifter.
- The opener should feel like a moderate single. Not easy, not hard.
- The opener exists to put a number on the board so the coach can choose the second attempt with information.

The opener is not the place for ambition. A missed opener compresses the rest of the day, drops the athlete's totalable bench or pull below what they should have had, and adds psychological cost to the next attempt.

## The second attempt

The second attempt is the lift that adds meaningful points to the total at a manageable risk.

Practical guidelines:

- The second is typically four to seven percent above the opener for squat and bench, three to five percent above the opener for deadlift.
- The second is at most an RPE 9, ideally an 8.5 in feel.
- The second should be a weight the athlete has hit cleanly in training, or a small step above the heaviest clean single in training, within the recent block.

The second attempt is the platform's primary scoring lift. If the opener went easily, the second can be ambitious. If the opener was harder than expected, the second should be conservative.

## The third attempt

The third attempt is where strategy lives.

Practical guidelines:

- The third is typically two to four percent above the second, depending on lift, federation rules, and what the second attempt looked like.
- The third may be a meet personal best, but it should not be a number the athlete has never tested in any form.
- The third should be chosen with full information: how the second moved, how the athlete looks between attempts, and where the lifter sits in the standings if placement matters.

The third attempt for placement is sometimes different from the third attempt for total. A coach who is chasing a podium spot may select a heavier third than a coach who is protecting a personal record total.

## Routing rules and decision logic

The coach's job between attempts:

### After the opener

- If the opener moved cleanly and at the expected speed, proceed with the planned second.
- If the opener was harder than expected at the bar, reduce the planned second by one to three percent.
- If the opener was a struggle, take a conservative second.

### After the second

- If the second was clean, the planned third can hold or move up slightly.
- If the second was a grind, reduce the planned third by one to three percent.
- If the second was missed, the strategy shifts: take the second again at a lower attempt, or take a third that secures a total.

### Bombing avoidance

The single most important attempt-selection rule: do not zero a lift. A made third attempt at a conservative weight is worth more than a missed third at the planned ambitious weight, almost always.

## Current-to-goal gap thinking

Map:

- Current training maxes and recent clean training singles.
- Goal total, with target attempts at each lift.
- Recovery curve from the taper and how the athlete looks in warm-ups.
- Federation rules on attempt changes and timing.
- Standings context if placement matters.

The opener is set conservatively from training data. The second and third are set with that opener result as the primary input.

## Application to BJJ and hybrid athletes

Most grapplers do not select powerlifting attempts in a meet context, but attempt selection logic transfers to any tested strength benchmark inside a hybrid program:

- Choose a conservative opener for any tested single.
- Use the opener to decide the next step.
- Avoid testing maximal singles on consecutive days or close to sport competition.

## Substitutions and contingencies

- If a planned opener cannot be hit cleanly in warm-ups, reduce the opener and protect a total.
- If the federation announces a rule call or red light that changes the lift's technical standard, reset the second and third to account for the new standard.
- If equipment issues, weigh-in surprises, or schedule changes alter the warm-up time, recalibrate the opener accordingly, almost always toward a slightly lighter opener.

## Coach and clinician review triggers

Pause and consider scratching when:

- A new sharp symptom appears in warm-ups.
- The athlete cannot complete a planned warm-up single cleanly at a load that should have been comfortable.
- Equipment or environmental issues create a safety question that a clinician or meet official should address.

Conservative routing: scratch the affected lift or drop the opener significantly. A made conservative total is recoverable. A platform injury is not always recoverable.

## Source-domain notes

Powerlifting practice, attempt selection methodology, autoregulation, coaching strategy, and contest-day logistics.

# 39. Powerlifting Peak Week: Tapering Volume Without Losing Readiness

## What Peak Week Actually Is

Peak week is not a deload. A deload reduces stress to recover from a block. Peak week reduces accumulated fatigue while preserving the neural sharpness, intramuscular coordination, and bar-speed feel built across the block. The goal is to express strength on the platform, not to keep building it. If you are still chasing PRs in the gym during peak week, you are still training, and you will likely under-perform the meet.

The working definition: peak week is the controlled removal of fatigue at a rate that exceeds the loss of fitness, so that readiness on meet day is the highest single point of the block.

## Prerequisites Before You Peak

Peak week only works if the block behind it earned a peak. Confirm the following before committing to a taper:

- The athlete hit prescribed top sets at RPE 8 or below in the last two heavy sessions of the block.
- Bar speed at meet-opener weight is unambiguous and crisp, not grinding.
- Sleep, body weight, and resting heart rate are inside the athlete's normal band.
- No new joint pain that altered technique in the last 14 days.
- The athlete's competition technique (commands, pause length, depth standard) has been rehearsed under load, not just in warm-ups.

If any of these are off, peak week becomes a damage-control window, not a sharpening window. That is a different intent and a different plan.

## The Core Tapering Logic

Volume comes down first and most. Intensity stays relatively high but is held below max effort. Frequency stays similar or drops by one session. Bar speed becomes the primary autoregulation signal.

A workable structure for a 7-day peak week into a Saturday meet:

### Monday (8 days out, last real session of the block end)

- Squat: work up to opener for a single at RPE 7.
- Bench: work up to opener minus 5 to 10 kg for a single at RPE 7.
- Light accessory: one upper back movement, one triceps movement, low volume.
- Total session time under 75 minutes.

### Tuesday or Wednesday (mid-week stimulus)

- Deadlift: work up to opener minus 10 to 15 kg for a single, then stop.
- Optional: light bench technique work, 3 sets of 3 at 60 percent, focus on leg drive and bar path.
- No back-down volume sets. No top sets above RPE 7.

### Thursday (skill maintenance)

- Squat: 2 to 3 singles at 60 to 70 percent, slow eccentric, fast concentric. Practice command timing.
- Bench: 2 to 3 singles at 60 to 70 percent. Practice the pause.
- Sled or light walking, optional.

### Friday (meet eve)

- Optional empty bar to mid 50 percent. Five to ten minutes of movement. Stop.
- Or full rest. Decide based on athlete preference, not principle.

### Saturday (meet day)

- Compete.

## Decision Rules for Bar Speed

Bar speed is the cleanest cheap signal during peak week. Use it as a routing tool:

- If opener speed on Monday is faster than block average: hold the plan.
- If opener speed is equal: hold the plan but drop one accessory.
- If opener speed is visibly slower at the same load: cut Tuesday or Wednesday's deadlift single to 70 percent for a double at RPE 6 and skip the mid-week back work entirely.
- If the athlete reports "heavy" sensation but speed looks fine on video: trust video, not feel. Peak-week perception of heaviness is normal.

## Fatigue Triggers That Change the Plan

Pause and review the plan if any of the following appear in the seven days before the meet:

- Two or more nights of sub-baseline sleep with no clear cause.
- Resting heart rate elevated 8 percent or more over a three-day rolling mean.
- New low-back stiffness on first morning movement that does not clear after warm-up by Wednesday.
- Bar path drift on bench at sub-maximal loads.
- Loss of appetite or unintended weight drop beyond planned cut.

When two or more triggers appear together: drop the second heavy session of the week, keep skill work only, and reassess Thursday. Do not chase one more "feel good" session. The most common peak-week error is one too many sessions.

## Powerlifting-Specific Application

Squat-dominant athletes generally benefit from earlier squat volume reduction, around 10 days out, with skill singles held longer. Bench-dominant athletes can hold a small dose of bench frequency deeper into peak week because bench tolerates volume better. Deadlift is the most fatigue-cost-heavy lift; the last heavy deadlift touch should usually be 10 to 12 days out, with peak week containing at most one moderate single. Sumo pullers often need an extra day of recovery before the meet compared to conventional pullers; consider moving the deadlift single to Monday instead of Tuesday.

## Hybrid and BJJ Athlete Considerations

For athletes who also train BJJ, peak week is not the time to test conditioning, spar at full intensity, or attend an open mat. Combat-sport stimulus during peak week introduces uncontrolled eccentric load, sympathetic activation, and skin-and-joint irritation that interfere with platform readiness.

Suggested routing:

- Drop live rolling 10 days out.
- Keep technical drilling without resistance through Wednesday of peak week.
- Stop all mat work by Thursday.
- Resume rolling no earlier than 5 to 7 days post-meet, with intensity restored gradually.

## Current-to-Goal Gap Before Choosing a Peak Style

Before locking the taper, walk through:

- Current best gym single on each lift, and the date.
- Goal attempt at the meet, with opener, second, and third defined.
- Recovery capacity: sleep, age, life stress, training age.
- Pain constraints: any joint that limited a session in the last block.
- Technical limiter: depth, lockout, leg drive, sumo break, hip hinge.

The wider the gap between gym numbers and goal numbers, the more conservative the taper should be. Athletes attempting a 5 percent jump over best gym single need a longer, gentler taper than athletes opening near a recent gym single.

## Coach and Clinician Review Triggers

Pause and review with the coach, or with a clinician where appropriate, if:

- Pain at a joint changes the lift pattern visibly on video.
- A nerve-type sensation (numbness, shooting, weakness that is new) appears at any load.
- Sleep collapses for three or more nights without clear cause.
- The athlete cannot complete an opener single at RPE 7 with normal speed.

These are routing signals, not diagnoses. The plan changes; the meet decision stays the athlete's.

## Source-Domain Notes

Powerlifting practice, tapering theory, neural readiness, autoregulation, velocity-based training, hybrid athlete interference management.

---

# 40. Attempt Selection for Powerlifting: Choosing Openers, Seconds, and Thirds

## The Frame for Attempt Selection

Attempt selection is risk management under partial information. The athlete does not know exact meet-day strength. The coach does not know the platform conditions, the judging standard for the day, or how the athlete will respond to the warm-up room. Attempt selection is the system that absorbs that uncertainty without sacrificing total.

The two failure modes are symmetric:

- Too conservative: the athlete finishes with all whites and leaves 10 to 20 kg on the platform. Total under-represents actual strength.
- Too aggressive: the athlete bombs a lift, total goes to zero on that lift, qualifying total is missed.

A 10/10 attempt selection plan prevents both, in that order. Avoiding the bomb-out is the higher priority.

## Opener Logic

The opener is the attempt the athlete could make on a bad day, with cold conditions, after a poor warm-up, with imperfect technique. The opener is not a number to be proud of. It is a number that buys total insurance.

Working anchors:

- Squat opener: a confident gym triple converted to a single, or roughly 90 to 93 percent of the realistic third attempt. Speed should look fast on warm-up video.
- Bench opener: 91 to 94 percent of realistic third. Pause discipline is the limiter, not load.
- Deadlift opener: 88 to 92 percent of realistic third for conventional. Sumo openers tend to feel slightly heavier off the floor; many sumo pullers open at 89 to 91 percent.

If the opener does not move with clear bar speed, the second is in question, and the third may need to be re-thought.

## Second Attempt Logic

The second attempt is the working attempt. It should represent the athlete's current realistic best single, in good conditions, with a clean opener behind it. The second attempt is where total is built.

Routing rules for the second:

- If the opener moved at expected or better speed: second is the planned number.
- If the opener moved noticeably slower than expected: the second drops by half of what it would have added. For example, planned jump of 10 kg becomes a 5 kg jump.
- If the opener was a grind: the second is the smallest legal jump that still increases total. Protect the lift; consider the third based on second performance.
- If the opener was missed: the second is a re-take of the opener. Do not chase.

## Third Attempt Logic

The third is built on information that did not exist before the opener and second moved. The third should be re-decided in the warm-up room and confirmed after the second.

Two coherent third-attempt styles:

- The total builder: a number that beats the second by a clear, achievable margin, with a high but not guaranteed probability of success. Often 2 to 4 percent above the second.
- The PR attempt: a number above any previous best, accepted as lower probability, only when total is secure and the meet position justifies risk.

Default to the total builder. PR attempts on the third are a coach decision, not an athlete decision, and they require a secure total and a specific reason.

## If/Then Routing for the Day

- If the opener missed on a technical fault (depth, command, hitched deadlift): re-take the opener exactly. Do not lower the load unless the coach saw a strength miss, not a technical miss.
- If the opener missed on strength: the second drops to a load the athlete has confidently hit in training in the last 4 to 6 weeks.
- If the second missed on strength: the third is the opener weight plus the smallest meaningful jump that still beats the opener. Total preservation is the priority.
- If the second missed on technical fault: re-take the second.
- If the second moved unexpectedly well: increase the third by no more than 2 to 3 percent over the originally planned third. Do not invent a new PR on the platform.

## Powerlifting-Specific Application

Federation matters. In federations with three commands on squat, opener confidence is more important because mis-timed lifts cost more attempts. In bench-only meets, opener percentage often runs lower because of the higher cost of a single bomb in a one-lift meet. In multi-ply contexts, equipment fit changes the math significantly and is outside the scope of this article.

The athlete's lifting style also matters:

- Grinders (long sticking points, slow concentric on heavy singles) need lower openers and smaller second-to-third jumps.
- Fast-twitch athletes with sharp lockouts tolerate larger jumps but should still respect a conservative opener.

## Hybrid and BJJ Athlete Considerations

Hybrid athletes often live further from their absolute strength ceiling because they share recovery with skill sport. For these athletes:

- Openers should be more conservative, near 92 to 94 percent of recent best gym single.
- Second attempts should match a recent confident gym single, not a stretch target.
- Third attempts are total-builder style by default. Save the PR chase for a strength-only block.

This is not a smaller goal. It is a goal that respects the dual demand of the training history.

## Current-to-Goal Gap Before Locking Attempts

Build the attempt sheet against:

- Current best gym single, with date, conditions, and equipment.
- Goal total, broken into squat, bench, deadlift contributions.
- Recovery state during peak week.
- Pain constraints that affect a specific lift, depth, or grip.
- Technical limiter that the athlete has not solved yet.

Attempt selection respects what the body has actually done recently, not what the spreadsheet says is "possible."

## Coach and Clinician Review Triggers

Pause and review the attempt sheet if:

- The athlete has not hit the planned opener as a clean single in the last 6 weeks.
- A pain pattern altered technique in the last block.
- The athlete is returning from time away from the platform and the gym numbers have not yet stabilized.

These are not reasons to skip the meet. They are reasons to write a more conservative sheet.

## Source-Domain Notes

Powerlifting practice, attempt selection theory, risk management, autoregulation, federation-specific judging standards, biomechanics of grinders versus speed lifters.

---

# 41. Testing 1RM Without Ruining the Next Training Block

## When 1RM Testing Earns Its Cost

A 1RM test is expensive. It uses neural capacity, joint tolerance, and recovery currency that could otherwise build the next block. Test 1RM only when the result will be used. Common legitimate uses:

- Setting accurate training maxes for a percentage-based block.
- Confirming readiness inside 12 weeks of a meet.
- Establishing a baseline after a long layoff or after a significant program change.
- Closing a coaching question that cannot be answered with rep-based estimation.

If the answer can come from a heavy double or triple plus a velocity check, that is the cheaper and usually better path. Default to the rep-based route unless one of the above conditions is present.

## Testing Modalities and Their Costs

Three reasonable testing modalities, in order of recovery cost:

### True 1RM Single

Highest neural and joint cost. Best ecological validity for meet prediction. Recovery typically 7 to 10 days for the lift tested, longer for deadlift.

### Top Single at RPE 9

Programmed as the heaviest single the athlete believes leaves one in the tank. Cost is lower than a true max because the athlete is not chasing the absolute ceiling. Recovery typically 5 to 7 days.

### Heavy Triple or Double Plus Estimation

A confident triple at RPE 8 to 9, run through a conservative estimator. Lowest cost. Recovery typically 3 to 5 days. Less accurate at the very top end, but adequate for most programming decisions.

Pick the lowest-cost option that answers the actual question.

## Prerequisites Before Testing

Confirm before scheduling a max:

- The block leading in was not in a fatigued phase.
- The week before the test contains reduced volume, similar to a mini taper.
- The athlete has hit a confident heavy single or top set inside the last 3 weeks.
- No new joint pain that altered technique recently.
- Sleep, body weight, and resting heart rate inside normal band.

If any of these are off, postpone. Testing a fatigued or compromised athlete delivers a fake number and a real recovery debt.

## A Defensible Testing Day Structure

A clean 1RM test for one lift looks like:

- General warm-up: 8 to 12 minutes.
- Empty bar: 2 sets of 5, technique focus.
- 40 percent: 5 reps.
- 55 percent: 3 reps.
- 70 percent: 2 reps.
- 80 percent: 1 rep.
- 88 percent: 1 rep.
- 93 percent: 1 rep, this is the opener-style single.
- 97 to 100 percent: planned top single.
- Optional 101 to 103 percent: only if the prior single moved with clear bar speed and no technical breakdown.

Stop on the first missed lift, slow grind, or technical breakdown. Do not chase.

## Decision Rules During the Test

- If the 93 percent single grinds: the planned top single is the test, no jump above it.
- If the 88 percent single moves slowly: drop the planned top to 95 percent and call it.
- If bar speed at the top single is unexpectedly fast: a 2 to 3 percent jump is allowed if the athlete is in a good state and the lift is technically clean.
- If technique breaks at any point (depth, bar path, lockout): the lift before the breakdown is the test result.

## Limiting Block Damage After Testing

The post-test window decides whether the test cost was worth paying.

For 5 to 7 days after a true 1RM test on a given lift:

- No top sets above RPE 7 on that lift.
- Volume on that lift at 60 to 70 percent, focused on technique.
- Light pulling or pressing accessories at low RPE.
- Sleep priority and protein adequacy.

For other lifts not tested: training proceeds at normal intensity, but expect some systemic carryover for 48 to 72 hours.

If two lifts were tested in the same week: assume a full reduced-intensity week behind both. Triple-lift testing in one week is rarely justified and routinely costs more than it informs.

## If/Then Routing After the Test

- If the test confirmed expected numbers: program the next block off the new training max.
- If the test exceeded expectations: program off the original projected number, not the new one. Banked progress is more durable than chased progress.
- If the test underperformed: do not raise volume to compensate. Examine sleep, stress, technical pattern, and block design before changing the training max.
- If the test produced a technical miss but the load was completable: re-test with technique fix in 7 to 10 days at the same load, not above it.

## Powerlifting-Specific Application

Do not test 1RM inside 8 weeks of a planned meet. Inside that window, use top singles at RPE 8 to 8.5 as readiness checks. The test in this window is the meet. Within 12 to 16 weeks of a meet, one 1RM test on one lift can be appropriate to calibrate the peaking block; usually that lift is the one with the most uncertainty.

## Hybrid and BJJ Athlete Considerations

Hybrid athletes should test 1RM less often. The recovery window after a true max competes directly with skill-sport intensity, and combat-sport stimulus inside the post-test window risks compounding fatigue and irritation at joints already taxed.

Routing for hybrid athletes:

- Default to top single at RPE 8.5 to 9 instead of a true 1RM.
- If a true 1RM is needed, reduce mat intensity for 5 to 7 days after.
- Avoid testing in the week before a competition or hard tournament prep block.

## Current-to-Goal Gap Before Testing

Walk through before scheduling a test:

- Current best confident single, with date.
- Goal number and the timeline to reach it.
- Recovery state and life-stress conditions over the last 4 weeks.
- Pain constraints that affect the lift to be tested.
- Technical limiter that has not been solved.

If the technical limiter is the bottleneck, testing measures the limiter, not strength. Solve the limiter first, then test.

## Coach and Clinician Review Triggers

Pause and review with the coach, or with a clinician where appropriate, before testing if:

- A pain pattern changed the lift's technique in the last block.
- A new nerve-type sensation appeared at any load.
- Sleep has been disrupted for more than a week.
- The athlete has not hit a confident heavy single in 4 to 6 weeks.

These do not rule testing out. They route the decision toward a lower-cost modality, or a delay.

## Source-Domain Notes

Powerlifting practice, maximal strength testing, RPE and velocity-based autoregulation, recovery monitoring, periodization, hybrid athlete interference management.

---

# 42. Meet Week Warm-Ups: Timing, Jumps, and Readiness Checks

## What Meet Warm-Ups Are For

The warm-up room is a controlled environment for one job: deliver the athlete to the platform with the opener loaded, the nervous system primed, and zero unnecessary fatigue. Warm-ups are not training. The temptation to add "just one more single" is the most common warm-up-room mistake.

A high-quality warm-up plan answers three questions before the meet starts:

- How many warm-up sets and what loads?
- What is the timing window between the last warm-up and the opener?
- What readiness checks confirm the athlete is ready for the platform?

## Structuring Warm-Up Sets

Default warm-up structure for a competition lift, scaled for time and athlete preference:

- Empty bar: 1 to 2 sets of 5, slow and clean.
- 40 percent of opener: 5 reps.
- 55 percent: 3 reps.
- 70 percent: 2 reps.
- 80 percent: 1 rep.
- 88 percent: 1 rep, this is the "almost opener" single.
- Optional 93 percent: 1 rep, only if the athlete wants a final touch.

Then the opener happens on the platform. Six to eight total warm-up sets is typical. More than ten is usually a mistake.

## Timing the Last Warm-Up

The last warm-up single should land somewhere between 6 and 15 minutes before the opener is called. The exact window depends on the athlete:

- Athletes who feel "cold" quickly: 6 to 8 minutes.
- Athletes with stable readiness: 8 to 12 minutes.
- Athletes who tend to over-rev and need calm: 10 to 15 minutes, with a single empty-bar touch around 4 to 5 minutes out.

The coach's job in the warm-up room is to read flight pace and back-calculate. A flight running fast may require collapsing two warm-up sets together. A flight running slow may require a re-warm with the empty bar.

## Re-Warming Strategies

If the flight stalls and the gap before the opener stretches past 15 minutes:

- Empty bar to 50 percent for one single, 4 to 6 minutes before the call.
- Stay moving between sets. Sitting cools the athlete.
- Do not re-take the last heavy single. The neural cost is real and the load does not need re-priming.

If the flight runs unexpectedly fast:

- Skip the 80 or 88 percent jump, not the lighter ones.
- A direct jump from 70 to opener is acceptable in an emergency. A direct jump from 55 to opener is not.

## Readiness Checks Before the Opener

Three quick checks before the athlete is sent to the platform:

- Bar speed on the last warm-up single, by eye and by video if available. Speed should match block-average bar speed at that percentage.
- Technical sharpness: clean depth on squat, clean leg drive and bar path on bench, clean break on deadlift.
- Subjective state: athlete reports "ready," not "hyped" and not "flat."

If two of three are off, the opener is in question. Pause and review with the coach.

## If/Then Routing in the Warm-Up Room

- If the 88 percent single moves slowly: the opener stands but the coach should be ready to call a smaller second-attempt jump.
- If the 88 percent single grinds: drop one warm-up step, take an empty bar to 60 percent, then go open. Reassess the rest of the day after the opener.
- If the athlete misses a warm-up single at sub-opener load: re-take it lighter. If still missed, pause and review with the coach about opener load.
- If a sharp pain appears in a warm-up set: stop the warm-up, walk, breathe, retry one lighter set. If pain persists, pause and review.

## Powerlifting-Specific Application

Squat warm-ups should include the actual competition bar if available, especially for athletes used to a different bar. Bench warm-ups should rehearse the pause command at every load above 70 percent; a clean pause is the most common warm-up-room oversight. Deadlift warm-ups should mirror the actual stance and grip used on the platform; switching grip mid-warm-up adds variance the athlete did not train for.

For deadlift specifically, the last warm-up before the platform tends to feel slightly heavier than expected because of the platform plates and the time gap. Plan for this. The 88 percent single should feel solid, not borderline.

## Hybrid and BJJ Athlete Considerations

Hybrid athletes often arrive at the platform with a different baseline arousal pattern than strength-only lifters. BJJ athletes are typically calm in competition environments, which is an advantage; the risk is under-arousal at the platform.

Routing for hybrid athletes:

- Slightly shorter warm-up gaps before the opener, often 6 to 10 minutes.
- One extra "wake-up" single at the empty bar in the final 5 minutes if subjective state reads flat.
- Avoid breath-work or meditation patterns in the final 10 minutes that pull arousal too low.

## Current-to-Goal Gap Before Setting the Warm-Up Plan

The warm-up plan respects the same gap analysis as the rest of meet week:

- Current best confident single, with date.
- Opener, second, and third targets.
- Recovery state through peak week.
- Pain constraints that affect any warm-up movement.
- Technical limiter that needs rehearsal in warm-ups.

If the technical limiter is depth, add one paused squat at 50 percent in the warm-up. If the limiter is leg drive, add a paused bench at 60 percent. Warm-ups can rehearse the limiter without taxing the athlete.

## Coach and Clinician Review Triggers

Pause and review during the warm-up room if:

- A new sharp pain appears at any load.
- A nerve-type sensation appears that was not present in training.
- The athlete cannot complete a sub-opener single with normal speed.
- The athlete reports a sudden change in subjective state (dizziness, lightheadedness, nausea) that does not clear with one minute of rest.

The opener decision can be re-routed quickly. The decision to compete at all is the athlete's, supported by the coach, supported where appropriate by a clinician on site.

## Source-Domain Notes

Powerlifting practice, warm-up structure, neural priming, autoregulation, velocity-based readiness assessment, hybrid athlete arousal management.
